# Function theory of the hexablock and applications to the tetrablock and Euclidean biball

**arXiv ID**: 2608.00819v1
**Authors**: Sourav Pal, Nitin Tomar
**Published**: 2026-08-01
**Categories**: math.CV, math.FA, math.OA
**Comments**: 34 Pages, This is just a primary draft, a revised version will appear soon
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.00819v1

## Abstract

The realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona problems are amongst the central themes in function theory of a domain in $\mathbb{C}^d$. The present work addresses these four problems for the hexablock $\mathbb{H}$, a domain in $\mathbb{C}^4$ arising in connection with a special case of $μ$-synthesis in $H^{\infty}$ control theory. We determine Schur-Agler class $SA(\mathbb{H})$ for $\mathbb{H}$ and find a realization formula for $\mathbb H$. With the help of this realization formula we state and prove interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems for the hexablock. As an application, we obtain analogous theorems for the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb{C}^2$. Moreover, we recover the existing same results for the tetrablock $\mathbb E$, another domain associated with the $μ$-synthesis, as consequences of the hexablock theory and in terms of the Schur-Agler class $SA(\mathbb{H})$ and admissible kernels on $\mathbb{H}$.

## Function theory of the hexablock and applications to the tetrablock and Euclidean biballSOURAV PAL AND NITIN TOMARMathematics Department, Indian Institute of Technology Bombay,
Powai, Mumbai - 400076, India.sourav@math.iitb.ac.inStatistics and Mathematics unit, Indian Statistical Institute Bangalore, Karnataka - 560059, India.tomarnitin414@gmail.com, nitin_pd@isibang.ac.in

## Abstract.

The realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona problems are amongst the central themes in function theory of a domain inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. The present work addresses these four problems for the hexablockℍ\mathbb{H}, a domain inℂ4\mathbb{C}^{4}arising in connection with a special case ofμ\mu-synthesis inH∞H^{\infty}control theory. We determine Schur-Agler classS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})forℍ\mathbb{H}and find a realization formula forℍ\mathbb{H}. With the help of this realization formula we state and prove interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems for the hexablock. As an application, we obtain analogous theorems for the Euclidean unit ball inℂ2\mathbb{C}^{2}. Moreover, we recover the existing same results for the tetrablock𝔼\mathbb{E}, another domain associated with theμ\mu-synthesis, as consequences of the hexablock theory and in terms of the Schur-Agler classS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})and admissible kernels onℍ\mathbb{H}.

## Key words and phrases:Hexablock, tetrablock, biball, realization, interpolation, extension, Toeplitz corona theorem

## 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification:32A70, 47A13, 47A57, 46E22, 47B32

## 1.Introduction

Throughout the article, all operators are bounded linear maps on complex separable Hilbert spaces. Letℬ​(ℋ)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})be the algebra of operators on a Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}. We write𝔻\mathbb{D}and𝕋\mathbb{T}for the unit disc and unit circle in the complex planeℂ\mathbb{C}, respectively, with center at the origin. LetσT​(T¯)\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})denote the Taylor joint spectrum of a commuting tupleT¯=(T1,…,Td)\underline{T}=(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{d})of operators. LetHol​(Ω)\mathrm{Hol}(\Omega)be the space of all holomorphic functions on a domainΩ\Omegainℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. We denote the Schur class ofΩ\OmegabyS​(Ω)={f∈Hol​(Ω):supz∈Ω|f​(z)|≤1}S(\Omega)=\{f\in\mathrm{Hol}(\Omega):\sup_{z\in\Omega}|f(z)|\leq 1\}, andH∞​(Ω)H^{\infty}(\Omega)denotes the space of all bounded holomorphic functions onΩ\Omega. The hexablock is a domain inℂ4\mathbb{C}^{4}given byℍ={(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℂ×𝔼:supα1,α2∈𝔻|z(1)​(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2|<1},\mathbb{H}=\left\{(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{E}:\sup_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{D}}\left|\frac{z^{(1)}\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}}{1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right|<1\right\},

where𝔼={(z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℂ3:1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2≠0​for all​α1,α2∈𝔻¯}\mathbb{E}=\{(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:\ 1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0\ \text{for all}\ \alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}\}. The domain𝔼\mathbb{E}is referred to as the tetrablock. These two domains arise naturally in connection with theμ\mu-synthesis problem in control engineering. To explain this connection, we recall the notion of the structured singular value from[32,38]. LetEEbe a linear subspace ofMd​(ℂ)M_{d}(\mathbb{C}), the space ofd×dd\times dcomplex matrices. The structured singular value ofA∈Md​(ℂ)A\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C})is defined byμE(A)=1/inf{∥X∥:X∈E,det(I−AX)=0},\mu_{E}(A)=1/\penalty 50\inf\{\|X\|:X\in E,\ \det(I-AX)=0\},

andμE​(A)=0\mu_{E}(A)=0if there does not existX∈EX\in Ewithdet(I−A​X)=0\det(I-AX)=0for allX∈EX\in E. The notion ofμE\mu_{E}comes up naturally in the study of systems with structured uncertainty and is important in theμ\mu-synthesis problem. Given distinct pointsα1,…,αn∈𝔻\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{D}and matricesB1,…,Bn∈Md​(ℂ)B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{C}), theμ\mu-synthesis problem asks for an analytic functionF:𝔻→Md​(ℂ)F:\mathbb{D}\to M_{d}(\mathbb{C})that interpolates the given data, i.e.,F​(αi)=BiF(\alpha_{i})=B_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq nand satisfiesμE​(F​(λ))≤1\mu_{E}(F(\lambda))\leq 1for allλ∈𝔻\lambda\in\mathbb{D}. Several well-known interpolation problems arise as special cases of theμ\mu-synthesis problem. For example, ifE=Md​(ℂ)E=M_{d}(\mathbb{C}), thenμE\mu_{E}is the operator norm and theμ\mu-synthesis problem becomes the matricial Nevanlinna-Pick interpolation problem[37]. Also, ifE={α​I:α∈ℂ}E=\{\alpha I:\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\}, thenμE\mu_{E}becomes the spectral radius and the correspondingμ\mu-synthesis problem reduces to the spectral Nevanlinna-Pick interpolation problem[10,29]. Interestingly, exploring different cases of theμ\mu-synthesis problem leads to different domains inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. For instance, whenEEis the linear subspace of all2×22\times 2scalar matrices, the associatedμ\mu-synthesis problem gives rise to the symmetrized bidisc given by𝔾2={(λ1+λ2,λ1​λ2)∈ℂ2:|λ1|,|λ2|<1}.\mathbb{G}_{2}=\{(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:|\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{2}|<1\}.

IfEEis the space of all diagonal matrices inM2​(ℂ)M_{2}(\mathbb{C}), then one obtains the tetrablock𝔼\mathbb{E}. Also, whenEEis taken to be the linear subspace of2×22\times 2upper triangular matrices with equal diagonal entries, the resulting domain is the pentablock given byℙ={(a21,tr​(A),det(A))∈ℂ3:A=[ai​j]∈M2​(ℂ),‖A‖<1}.\mathbb{P}=\{(a_{21},\mathrm{tr}(A),\det(A))\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:A=[a_{ij}]\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\|A\|<1\}.

The domains𝔾2,𝔼\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathbb{E}andℙ\mathbb{P}were introduced in[8],[1]and[4], respectively. For more details on the connections of these three domains withμ\mu-synthesis, we also refer the readers to Chapter 1 of[26]. Besides from complex-geometric and function-theoretic point of view, the domains𝔾2\mathbb{G}_{2},𝔼\mathbb{E}andℙ\mathbb{P}have been extensively studied from operator theoretic perspective in[9,11,23],[22]and[44], respectively. Motivated by the origins of these three domains, the authors of[26]considered the linear subspaceEEconsisting of all upper triangular matrices inM2​(ℂ)M_{2}(\mathbb{C}), which gives rise to the domainℍ\mathbb{H}. To see this, let us denote byμhexa\mu_{\text{hexa}}the corresponding functionμE\mu_{E}and defineℍμ={(a21,a11,a22,det(A)):A=[ai​j]∈M2​(ℂ),μhexa​(A)<1}.\mathbb{H}_{\mu}=\{(a_{21},a_{11},a_{22},\det(A)):A=[a_{ij}]\in M_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mu_{\text{hexa}}(A)<1\}.

The setℍμ\mathbb{H}_{\mu}is calledμ\mu-hexablock by the authors of[26]. Unlike theμ\mu-hexablockℍμ\mathbb{H}_{\mu}, the hexablockℍ\mathbb{H}is a domain inℂ4\mathbb{C}^{4}that satisfiesℍ=int​(ℍ¯μ)\mathbb{H}=\text{int}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}_{\mu}). The setℍ\mathbb{H}is referred to as the hexablock, motivated by the geometry ofℍ∩ℝ4\mathbb{H}\cap\mathbb{R}^{4}, which is a bounded set with boundary consisting of four extreme sets and two hypersurfaces.

In this article, we establish realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems for the domainℍ\mathbb{H}. As applications of our main results, we obtain analogous theorems for the biball𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}, and we also recover the corresponding results for the tetrablock𝔼\mathbb{E}established in[39]. However, our characterizations of the realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems for𝔼\mathbb{E}are formulated in terms of admissible kernels onℍ\mathbb{H}, thereby providing a unified framework for these results on both𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}and𝔼\mathbb{E}. These four problems arise naturally in the function theory of domains inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}and have been studied extensively over the past several decades. We begin with a brief overview of these topics and recall some of the domains inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}for which the corresponding results are known. The study of interpolation on domains inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}typically begins with a characterization of the class of functions to which the interpolating functions belong. Such a characterization is commonly referred to as a realization theorem. For the unit disc𝔻\mathbb{D}, the realization theorem (see[6]) states thatf∈S​(𝔻)f\in S(\mathbb{D})if and only if there exist a Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}and a unitary operatorV=[ABCD]:ℂ⊕ℋ→ℂ⊕ℋsuch thatf​(z)=A+z​B​(Iℋ−z​D)−1​C.V=\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}:\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{H}\quad\text{such that}\quad f(z)=A+zB(I_{\mathcal{H}}-zD)^{-1}C.

Realization formulas of this type have been established for several domains, including the annulus[34]and the bidisc[3,6]. In higher dimensions, such as the polydisc𝔻d\mathbb{D}^{d}ford≥3d\geq 3, not every Schur class function admits a realization. However, an important subclass, known as the Schur-Agler class, admits such a realization theorem[6]. This framework has been further generalized to abstract settings where the domain is replaced by a set equipped with a family of test functions[34,33,20]. Realization theorems have also been obtained for domains arising fromμ\mu-synthesis, such as the symmetrized bidisc[12,24], the tetrablock[39]and the pentablock[46]. Once a realization theorem is established, one can address an interpolation problem on a domain inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. The classical Nevanlinna-Pick interpolation theorem[43,48]states that given distinct pointsz1,…,znz_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}in𝔻\mathbb{D}and pointsλ1,…,λn\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}in𝔻¯\overline{\mathbb{D}}, there exists a holomorphic functionf:𝔻→𝔻¯f:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{\mathbb{D}}satisfyingf​(zi)=λif(z_{i})=\lambda_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq nif and only if the Pick matrix[1−λi​λ¯j1−zi​z¯j]i,j=1n\left[\frac{1-\lambda_{i}\overline{\lambda}_{j}}{1-z_{i}\overline{z}_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{n}

is positive semi-definite. The above positivity condition of the Pick matrix is same as the positive semi-definiteness of[(1−λi​λ¯j)​kS​(zi,zj)]i,j=1n\left[(1-\lambda_{i}\overline{\lambda}_{j})k_{S}(z_{i},z_{j})\right]_{i,j=1}^{n}, wherekS​(z,w)=(1−z​w¯)−1k_{S}(z,w)=(1-z\overline{w})^{-1}is the Szegő kernel on𝔻\mathbb{D}. For more general domains, interpolation problems often require families of admissible kernels, as seen in the work of Abrahamse[2]for multiply connected domains and Agler-McCarthy for the bidisc[5,6]. In this direction, one considers the following interpolation problem: LetΩ⊂ℂd\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}be a domain. Given distinct pointsz1,…,znz_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}inΩ\Omegaand pointsλ1,…,λn\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}in𝔻¯\overline{\mathbb{D}}, when does there exist a holomorphic functionf:Ω→ℂf:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}withf​(zi)=λif(z_{i})=\lambda_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n? One usually imposes additional norm conditions onff. Such problems have been studied for several domains in the literature. An interested reader is referred to[31,40]for the polydisc𝔻d\mathbb{D}^{d}and[21,30,36,41]for the Euclidean unit ball𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}. In addition, various interpolation problems in the class of Schur multipliers on𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}have been extensively studied in[18,27,49]. The interpolation theorems for𝔾2\mathbb{G}_{2},𝔼\mathbb{E}andℙ\mathbb{P}were presented in[12,24],[39]and[46], respectively. Finally, interpolation results often lead naturally to extension theorems. LetVVbe a subset of a domainΩ⊂ℂd\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}. A functionf:V→ℂf:V\to\mathbb{C}is said to be holomorphic if it extends to a holomorphic function in a neighbourhood ofVV. The norm-preserving extension problem asks for necessary and sufficient conditions under which every bounded holomorphic function onVVadmits an extension to a function inH∞​(Ω)H^{\infty}(\Omega)without increasing its norm. Such extension results are established for several domains including the bidisc[7], the symmetrized bidisc[24], the tetrablock[39]and the pentablock[46].

Another important application of realization theorems for domains inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}is the study of Toeplitz corona theorems associated with them. The classical corona problem for the Banach algebraH∞​(𝔻)H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D})was solved by Carleson[28], who proved that ifφ1,…,φd∈H∞​(𝔻)\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{d}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D})satisfy(1.1)supz∈𝔻[|φ1​(z)|2+…+|φn​(z)|2]≥ϵ2\sup_{z\in\mathbb{D}}\left[|\varphi_{1}(z)|^{2}+\dotsc+|\varphi_{n}(z)|^{2}\right]\geq\epsilon^{2}

for someϵ>0\epsilon>0, then there existf1,…,fd∈H∞​(𝔻)f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D})such thatφ1​f1+…+φd​fd=1.\varphi_{1}f_{1}+\dotsc+\varphi_{d}f_{d}=1.Arveson[16]established an operator-theoretic analogue, now known as the Toeplitz corona theorem, by replacing (1.1) with the operator inequalityTφ1​Tφ1∗+…+Tφd​Tφd∗≥ϵ2​I.T_{\varphi_{1}}T_{\varphi_{1}}^{*}+\dotsc+T_{\varphi_{d}}T_{\varphi_{d}}^{*}\geq\epsilon^{2}I.Here,TφiT_{\varphi_{i}}is the Toeplitz operator with symbolφi\varphi_{i}for1≤i≤d1\leq i\leq d. Toeplitz corona theorems have been established for several domains including the bidisc[5], the Euclidean unit ball and the polydisc[13], the symmetrized bidisc[25], the tetrablock[39]and the pentablock[47].

The principal domains of interest in this article are the hexablockℍ\mathbb{H}, the tetrablock𝔼\mathbb{E}and the biball𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}. We present realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems forℍ\mathbb{H}in Section2. To begin with, we introduce the class𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}of commuting operator quadruplesT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})satisfying‖T3‖<1,‖(α​T4−T2)​(α​T3−I)−1‖<1\|T_{3}\|<1,\|(\alpha T_{4}-T_{2})(\alpha T_{3}-I)^{-1}\|<1and‖(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)​T1​(I−α1​T2−α2​T3+α1​α2​T4)−1‖<1\|\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}T_{1}(I-\alpha_{1}T_{2}-\alpha_{2}T_{3}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}T_{4})^{-1}\|<1

for allα∈𝔻¯2\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}and(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}. The motivation behind considering𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}comes from the definition ofℍ\mathbb{H}, which is explained in Section2. Also, any operator quadruple in𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}has its joint spectrum contained inℍ\mathbb{H}and so, one can define the functional calculusf​(T¯)f(\underline{T})for everyT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}andf∈Hol​(ℍ)f\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{H}). In light of this observation, we define the Schur-Agler class forℍ\mathbb{H}as the class given byS​A​(ℍ)={f∈Hol​(ℍ):‖f​(T¯)‖≤1for allT¯∈𝔐ℍ}.SA(\mathbb{H})=\left\{f\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{H}):\ \text{$\|f(\underline{T})\|\leq 1$ for all $\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}$}\right\}.

Our realization theorem forℍ\mathbb{H}is precisely the characterization of functions inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})stated as in Theorem2.7. As mentioned earlier, realization theorem plays a pivotal role to obtain a corresponding Nevanlinna-Pick type interpolation theorem. In the same spirit, we capitalize the realization theorem for Schur-Agler classS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})and obtain in Theorem2.8necessary and sufficient conditions for an interpolation problem onℍ\mathbb{H}, where the interpolating function belongs toS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H}). Next, we turn our attention to the extension problem onℍ\mathbb{H}. To do so, we introduce in Section2the classQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}consisting of all commuting quadruplesT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})of Hilbert space operators withσT​(T¯)⊆ℍ\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})\subseteq\mathbb{H}such that‖T3‖≤1,‖(α​T4−T2)​(α​T3−I)−1‖≤1\|T_{3}\|\leq 1,\|(\alpha T_{4}-T_{2})(\alpha T_{3}-I)^{-1}\|\leq 1and‖(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)​T1​(I−α1​T2−α2​T3+α1​α2​T4)−1‖≤1\|\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}T_{1}(I-\alpha_{1}T_{2}-\alpha_{2}T_{3}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}T_{4})^{-1}\|\leq 1

for allα∈𝔻¯2\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}and(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}. Following the terminologies in[42], we refer toQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}as the quantum hexablock. By quantization, we mean that scalar variables are replaced by commuting operators satisfying analogous norm inequalities. Finally, we define the notion of subordination to a subsetWWofℍ\mathbb{H}and consider the operator quadruples inQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}that are subordinate toWW. With these notions in place, we present our extension theorem as Theorem2.9. In particular, it is proved that ifW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}andffis a bounded function onWWthat is holomorphic onWW, then there existsg∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})withg|W=fg|_{W}=f,‖g‖∞,ℍ=‖f‖∞,W\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}=\|f\|_{\infty,W}andg/‖f‖∞,W∈S​A​(ℍ)g/\|f\|_{\infty,W}\in SA(\mathbb{H})if and only if‖f​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,W\|f(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for everyT¯∈Q​ℍ\underline{T}\in Q\mathbb{H}subordinate toWW. We also establish Toeplitz corona theorem forℍ\mathbb{H}as Theorem2.19.

Next, we present in Section3the realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona type theorems on the biball𝔹2={(z(1),z(2))∈ℂ2:|z(1)|2+|z(2)|2<1}\mathbb{B}_{2}=\{(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:|z^{(1)}|^{2}+|z^{(2)}|^{2}<1\}as applications of the corresponding theorems associated withℍ\mathbb{H}. The starting point in this direction is the fact that(z(1),z(2))∈𝔹2(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})\in\mathbb{B}_{2}if and only if(z(1),z(2),0,0)∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},0,0)\in\mathbb{H}. Moreover, if(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}, then(z(1),z(2))∈𝔹2(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})\in\mathbb{B}_{2}. Consequently, the domain𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}can be embedded holomorphically inℍ\mathbb{H}. We refer to Chapter 10 in[26]for further details on the connection of𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}withℍ\mathbb{H}. Motivated by this, we consider the class𝔐𝔹2\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}={(T1,T2):(T1,T2,0,0)∈𝔐ℍ}.\displaystyle=\{(T_{1},T_{2}):(T_{1},T_{2},0,0)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}\}.

For an operator pair(T1,T2)∈𝔐𝔹2(T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}, it follows thatσT​(T1,T2)⊆𝔹2\sigma_{T}(T_{1},T_{2})\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{2}and so, one can defineg​(T1,T2)g(T_{1},T_{2})for allg∈Hol​(𝔹2)g\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}). We then consider a subclass of the Schur classS​(𝔹2)S(\mathbb{B}_{2})give byℭ​(𝔹2)={g∈Hol​(𝔹2):‖g​(T1,T2)‖≤1​for all​(T1,T2)∈𝔐𝔹2}.\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2})=\{g\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}):\|g(T_{1},T_{2})\|\leq 1\ \text{for all}\ (T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}\}.

It is proved in Theorem3.2thatg∈ℭ​(𝔹2)g\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2})if and only ifg∘θℍ→𝔹2∈S​A​(ℍ)g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}\in SA(\mathbb{H}), whereθℍ→𝔹2\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}is the map give byθℍ→𝔹2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))=(z(1),z(2))\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)}). Consequently, the realization theorem forS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})yields a realization theorem for functions inℭ​(𝔹2)\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2}). In an analogous manner, we obtain interpolation extension and Toeplitz corona type results on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}in Theorems3.3,3.5and3.6, respectively, from the corresponding theorems onℍ\mathbb{H}. In Section4, we recover the realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems on the tetrablock𝔼\mathbb{E}, where the realization theorems for functions in the Schur-Agler classS​A​(𝔼)SA(\mathbb{E})is now presented in terms of functions inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H}). Similarly, the interpolation theorem is resolved in terms of functions and kernels associated with the hexablock framework. To begin with, note that if(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}, then(z(2),z(3),z(4))∈𝔼(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}. Moreover,(z(1),z(2),z(3))∈𝔼(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)})\in\mathbb{E}if and only if(0,z(1),z(2),z(3))∈ℍ(0,z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)})\in\mathbb{H}. Therefore, the domain𝔼\mathbb{E}can be holomorphically embedded insideℍ\mathbb{H}. As an application of this, we prove in Theorem4.1thatg∈S​A​(𝔼)g\in SA(\mathbb{E})if and only ifg∘θℍ→𝔼∈S​A​(ℍ)g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{E}}\in SA(\mathbb{H}), whereθℍ→𝔼​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))=(z(1),z(2),z(3))\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{E}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)}). This provides a realization theorem for functions inS​A​(𝔼)SA(\mathbb{E})through the realization theorem for functions inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})presented in Section2. In a similar way, we present in Theorems4.2,4.4and4.5the interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems on𝔼\mathbb{E}, respectively, as applications of the corresponding results onℍ\mathbb{H}proved in Section2.

## 2.Realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems onℍ\mathbb{H}

In this section, we introduce the Schur-Agler class for the hexablockℍ\mathbb{H}and establish a realization theorem for functions in this class. Also, we prove an interpolation theorem onℍ\mathbb{H}with interpolating functions in the Schur-Agler class forℍ\mathbb{H}. Recall that the hexablock is given byℍ={(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℂ×𝔼:supα1,α2∈𝔻|z(1)​(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2|<1},\mathbb{H}=\left\{(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{E}:\sup_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{D}}\left|\frac{z^{(1)}\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}}{1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\right|<1\right\},

where𝔼\mathbb{E}itself is a domain inℂ3\mathbb{C}^{3}defined as𝔼={(z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℂ3:1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2≠0​for all​α1,α2∈𝔻¯}.\mathbb{E}=\left\{(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\neq 0\ \text{for all}\ \alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}\right\}.

The domain𝔼\mathbb{E}is called the tetrablock. The authors of[1]gave the following characterization of𝔼\mathbb{E}:(2.1)(z(2),z(3),z(4))∈𝔼if and only if​|z(3)|<1​and​|Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))|<1​for all​α∈𝔻¯,(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}\ \ \text{if and only if}\ |z^{(3)}|<1\ \text{and}\ |\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})|<1\ \text{for all}\ \alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}},

whereΨ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))=(α​z(4)−z(2))/(α​z(3)−1)\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=(\alpha z^{(4)}-z^{(2)})/\penalty 50(\alpha z^{(3)}-1). Capitalizing the above description of𝔼\mathbb{E}, the authors of[26]proved thatz=(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍz=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}if and only if(2.2)|z(3)|<1,|Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))|<1and|ψα1,α2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))|<1|z^{(3)}|<1,\quad|\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})|<1\quad\text{and}\quad|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})|<1

for allα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}, whereψα1,α2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))=z(1)​(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2.\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=\frac{z^{(1)}\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}}{1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}.

An interesting fact about the domains𝔼\mathbb{E}andℍ\mathbb{H}is that both are quasi-balanced domains, i.e.,(r​w(2),r​w(3),r2​w(4))∈𝔼(rw^{(2)},rw^{(3)},r^{2}w^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}and(r​z(1),r​z(2),r​z(3),r2​z(4))∈ℍ(rz^{(1)},rz^{(2)},rz^{(3)},r^{2}z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}for0≤r≤1,(w(2),w(3),w(4))∈𝔼0\leq r\leq 1,(w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}and(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}. Motivated by the description ofℍ\mathbb{H}as in (2.2), we define for every pointz=(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍz=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}the following functions:E​(z):𝔻¯2→ℂ,E​(z)​(α1,α2)=ψα1,α2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))=z(1)​(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2;\displaystyle E(z):\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\to\mathbb{C},\quad E(z)(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=\frac{z^{(1)}\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}}{1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}};(2.3)e​(z):𝔻¯→ℂ,e​(z)​(α)=Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))=α​z(4)−z(2)α​z(3)−1.\displaystyle e(z):\overline{\mathbb{D}}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad e(z)(\alpha)=\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=\frac{\alpha z^{(4)}-z^{(2)}}{\alpha z^{(3)}-1}.

It is evident thatE​(z)E(z)ande​(z)e(z)are continuous functions on𝔻¯2\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}and𝔻¯\overline{\mathbb{D}}, respectively, for everyz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H}. Furthermore, the supremum norms‖E​(z)‖∞,𝔻¯2,‖e​(z)‖∞,𝔻¯<1\|E(z)\|_{\infty,\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}},\|e(z)\|_{\infty,\overline{\mathbb{D}}}<1for everyz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H}. We now introduce an operator-theoretic analog of the inequalities provided in (2.2). Let(T1,T2,T3,T4)(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})be a commuting quadruple of operators acting on a Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}, and let‖T3‖<1\|T_{3}\|<1. for allα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}. The class of commuting triples of operators given by𝔐𝔼={(T2,T3,T4):‖T3‖<1,‖Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4)‖<1​for all​α∈𝔻¯}\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}=\left\{(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4}):\|T_{3}\|<1,\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1\ \text{for all}\ \alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}\right\}

was introduced by the authors of[39]. The inequalities in𝔐𝔼\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}may be viewed as an operator theoretic analog of the inequalities in (2.1), where the scalars in𝔼\mathbb{E}are replaced in (2.1) by commuting operator triples satisfying similar norm bounds. The class𝔐𝔼\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}contains𝔼\mathbb{E}in the sense that(z(2)​I,z(3)​I,z(4)​I)∈𝔐𝔼(z^{(2)}I,z^{(3)}I,z^{(4)}I)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}for all(z(2),z(3),z(4))∈𝔼(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}, which follows from (2.1).

We now show that the joint spectrum of an operator triple in𝔐𝔼\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}is contained in𝔼\mathbb{E}. This inclusion has been established in[39]. We provide an alternative proof here. Let(T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐𝔼(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}and let(w(2),w(3),w(4))∈σT​(T2,T3,T4)(w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})\in\sigma_{T}(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4}). Forα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}, we have that‖Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4)‖<1\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1. It now follows from spectral mapping principle thatΨ​(α,w(2),w(3),w(4))∈Ψ​(α,σT​(T2,T3,T4))=σ​(Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4))⊆𝔻.\Psi(\alpha,w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})\in\Psi(\alpha,\sigma_{T}(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4}))=\sigma(\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4}))\subseteq\mathbb{D}.

By (2.1),(w(2),w(3),w(4))∈𝔼(w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})\in\mathbb{E}andσT​(T2,T3,T4)⊆𝔼\sigma_{T}(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\subseteq\mathbb{E}. So, one can definef​(T2,T3,T4)f(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})for everyf∈Hol​(𝔼)f\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{E})and(T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐𝔼(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}via the holomorphic functional calculus. The Schur-Agler class for𝔼\mathbb{E}is defined asS​A​(𝔼)={f∈Hol(𝔼):‖f​(T2,T3,T4)‖<1​for all​(T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐𝔼}.SA(\mathbb{E})=\{f\in\text{Hol$(\mathbb{E})$}:\|f(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1\ \text{for all}\ (T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}\}.

Next, for(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}, consider the polynomialp​(z(2),z(3),z(4))=1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2.p(z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}.

for all(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}and commuting quadruples(T1,T2,T3,T4)(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})of Hilbert space operators such that(T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐𝔼(T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{E}}. In view of this, we introduce the class𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}consisting of all commuting quadruplesT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})of Hilbert space operators such that‖T3‖<1,‖Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4)‖<1and‖ψα1,α2​(T1,T2,T3,T4)‖<1\|T_{3}\|<1,\quad\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1\quad\text{and}\quad\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1

for allα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}. We note the following basic properties of𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}, which will be used subsequently in this section.
- (1)

𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}containsℍ\mathbb{H}in the sense that(z(1)​I,z(2)​I,z(3)​I,z(4)​I)∈𝔐ℍ(z^{(1)}I,z^{(2)}I,z^{(3)}I,z^{(4)}I)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}for all(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}.

It is evident to see that(T1,T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐ℍ(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}if and only if(T1∗,T2∗,T3∗,T4∗)∈𝔐ℍ(T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T_{3}^{*},T_{4}^{*})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}.

and by (2.2),(w(1),w(2),w(3),w(4))∈ℍ(w^{(1)},w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}. This ensures that the functional calculusf​(T¯)f(\underline{T})is well-defined for anyf∈Hol​(ℍ)f\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{H})andT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}.

## Definition 2.1.

For a non-empty setYY, a mapf:Y×Y→ℂf:Y\times Y\to\mathbb{C}is said to be positive semi-definite if∑i,j=1nc¯i​cj​f​(yi,yj)≥0\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\overline{c}_{i}c_{j}f(y_{i},y_{j})\geq 0for every{y1,…,yn}⊆Y,{c1,…,cn}⊂ℂ\{y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n}\}\subseteq Y,\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{C}andn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}. A positive semi-definite functionk:ℍ×ℍ→ℂk:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}is referred to as a weak kernel onℍ\mathbb{H}. In addition, ifk​(z,z)≠0k(z,z)\neq 0for allz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H}, we say thatkkis a kernel onℍ\mathbb{H}. LetFFbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}andK⊆ℂmK\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}be compact. A functionξ:F×F→C​(K)∗\xi:F\times F\to C(K)^{*}is said to be a positive kernel with values in the dualC​(K)∗C(K)^{*}of the spaceC​(K)C(K)of complex-valued continuous functions onKKif the following holds:∑i,j=1𝑛​c¯i​cj​ξ​(zi,zj)​(fi​f¯j)≥0\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\overline{c}_{i}c_{j}\xi(z_{i},z_{j})(f_{i}\overline{f}_{j})\geq 0

for alln∈ℕ,{z1,…,zn}⊆F,{c1,…,cn}⊂ℂn\in\mathbb{N},\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subseteq F,\{c_{1},\dotsc,c_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{C}and{f1,…,fn}⊆C​(K)\{f_{1},\dotsc,f_{n}\}\subseteq C(K). A kernel (weak kernel)k:ℍ×ℍ→ℂk:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}is said to beadmissible(weakly admissible) if the following hold:
- (1)

(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​k​(z,w)≽0\left(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)}\right)k(z,w)\succcurlyeq 0;
- (2)

(1−Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))​Ψ​(α,w(2),w(3),w(4))¯)​k​(z,w)≽0\left(1-\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\overline{\Psi(\alpha,w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})}\right)k(z,w)\succcurlyeq 0for allα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}};
- (3)

(1−ψα1,α2(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))ψα1,α2(w(1),w(2),w(3),w(4)¯))k(z,w)≽0\left(1-\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\overline{\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(w^{(1)},w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)}})\right)k(z,w)\succcurlyeq 0for all(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}.

We writef≽0f\succcurlyeq 0to denote thatffis a positive semi-definite function. We denote byC​(K)F+C(K)_{F}^{+}the set of all positive kernels with values inC​(K)∗C(K)^{*}, and the set of all positive semi-definite functionsδ:F×F→ℂ\delta:F\times F\to\mathbb{C}is denoted byℂF+\mathbb{C}_{F}^{+}. The class of admissible kernels onℍ\mathbb{H}is denoted byA​K​(ℍ)AK(\mathbb{H}). With these definitions in place, we now present the following result.

## Proposition 2.2.

LetFFbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}and letKKbe a compact subset ofℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}. Ifξ∈C​(K)F+\xi\in C(K)_{F}^{+}, then there exist a Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}, a functionL:F→ℬ​(C​(K),ℋ)L:F\to\mathcal{B}(C(K),\mathcal{H})and a unital∗*-representationρ:C​(K)→ℬ​(ℋ)\rho:C(K)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})such thatξ​(z,w)​(f​g¯)=⟨L​(z)​f,L​(w)​g⟩andL​(z)​(f​g)=ρ​(f)​L​(z)​(g)\xi(z,w)(f\overline{g})=\langle L(z)f,L(w)g\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad L(z)(fg)=\rho(f)L(z)(g)

for allf,g∈C​(K)f,g\in C(K)andz,w∈Fz,w\in F.

## Proof.

Define the mapξ′:(F×C​(K))×(F×C​(K))→ℂ\xi^{\prime}:(F\times C(K))\times(F\times C(K))\to\mathbb{C}asξ′​((z,h1),(w,h2))=ξ​(z,w)​(h1​h¯2)\xi^{\prime}((z,h_{1}),(w,h_{2}))=\xi(z,w)(h_{1}\overline{h}_{2}). It is not difficult to see thatξ′\xi^{\prime}is a positive definite function onF×C​(K)F\times C(K). It follows from Theorem 2.53 in[6]that there is a Hilbert spaceℋ\mathcal{H}and a vector-valued mapg:F×C​(K)→ℋg:F\times C(K)\to\mathcal{H}such that⟨g​(z,h1),g​(w,h2)⟩ℋ=ξ′​((z,h1),(w,h2))=ξ​(z,w)​(h1​h¯2)\langle g(z,h_{1}),g(w,h_{2})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\xi^{\prime}((z,h_{1}),(w,h_{2}))=\xi(z,w)(h_{1}\overline{h}_{2})

for allh1,h2∈C​(K)h_{1},h_{2}\in C(K)andz,w∈Fz,w\in F. Indeed, one can choose the Hilbert spaceℋ=span¯​{g​(z,h):z∈F,h∈C​(K)}\mathcal{H}=\overline{\text{span}}\{g(z,h):z\in F,h\in C(K)\}. for allz∈F,h∈C​(K)z\in F,h\in C(K). Consider the mapρ:C​(K)→ℬ​(ℋ)\rho:C(K)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})given byρ​(h1)​g​(z,h2)=g​(z,h1​h2)\rho(h_{1})g(z,h_{2})=g(z,h_{1}h_{2}). For a non-empty setYY, a mapf:Y×Y→ℂf:Y\times Y\to\mathbb{C}is said to beself-adjointonYYiff​(z,w)=f​(w,z)¯f(z,w)=\overline{f(w,z)}for allz,w∈Yz,w\in Y. Our next result presents a description of self-adjoint functions onℍ\mathbb{H}such that their product with every admissible kernel is positive semi-definite. As before, we follow the methods developed in Section 3 of[34]and Section 5 of[33]. LetFFbe a finite subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}with cardinality|F||F|. LetℭF\mathfrak{C}_{F}be the collection of matrices of the form[ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w)]z,w∈F,\left[\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w)\right]_{z,w\in F},

whereξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)F+,∇∈C​(𝔻¯)F+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{F},\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{F}andδ∈ℂF+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{F}^{+}. Following the same arguments as in Lemma 3.4 of[34], we observe thatℭF\mathfrak{C}_{F}is a closed cone inM|F|​(ℂ)M_{|F|}(\mathbb{C}), the space of|F|×|F||F|\times|F|matrices. Furthermore, any positive semi-definite matrix inM|F|​(ℂ)M_{|F|}(\mathbb{C})belongs toℭF+\mathfrak{C}_{F}^{+}and hence, has non-empty interior. To see this, letP=[P​(z,w)]z,w∈FP=[P(z,w)]_{z,w\in F}be a positive semi-definite matrix. Defineδ​(z,w)=P​(z,w)​δ0​(z,w)\delta(z,w)=P(z,w)\delta_{0}(z,w), whereδ0​(z,w)=11−z(3)​w¯(3)\displaystyle\delta_{0}(z,w)=\frac{1}{1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)}}forz,w∈Fz,w\in F. A simple computation shows thatδ∈ℂF+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{F}^{+}and so,[P​(z,w)]z,w∈F=[δ​(z,w)​(1−z(3)​w¯(3))]z,w∈F∈ℭF.[P(z,w)]_{z,w\in F}=[\delta(z,w)(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})]_{z,w\in F}\in\mathfrak{C}_{F}.

We refer the readers to[34,33]for a more detailed discussion in a broader setting.

Since a representation on a larger set restricts naturally to any smaller subset, an application of the same reasoning as in Theorem 11.5 of[6], together with Kurosh’s theorem (see[15], Page 7) gives the asserted conclusion.

We now define a unitary colligation associated withℍ\mathbb{H}.

## Definition 2.4.

A functionf:ℍ→ℂf:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}is said to be associated to aunitary colligationif there exist
- (1)

Hilbert spacesℋ1,ℋ2\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}andℋ3\mathcal{H}_{3},
- (2)

unital∗*-representationsρ1:C​(𝔻¯2)→ℬ​(ℋ1),ρ2:C​(𝔻¯)→ℬ​(ℋ2)\rho_{1}:C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\rho_{2}:C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{2})and
- (3)

a unitaryV=[ABCD]:ℂ⊕ℋ→ℂ⊕ℋV=\begin{bmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{bmatrix}:\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{H}\to\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathcal{H}, whereℋ=ℋ1⊕ℋ2⊕ℋ3\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{3}

such thatf​(z)=A+B​X​(z)​(Iℋ−D​X​(z))−1​Cf(z)=A+BX(z)(I_{\mathcal{H}}-DX(z))^{-1}C, whereX​(z)=[ρ1​(E​(z))000ρ2​(e​(z))000z(3)​Iℋ3](z=(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍ).X(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}(E(z))&0&0\\
0&\rho_{2}(e(z))&0\\
0&0&z^{(3)}I_{\mathcal{H}_{3}}\end{bmatrix}\quad(z=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}).

The class of functionsf:ℍ→ℂf:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}associated to a unitary colligation is denoted byU​C​(ℍ)UC(\mathbb{H}).

It follows from the definition ofU​C​(ℍ)UC(\mathbb{H})thatU​C​(ℍ)⊆S​(ℍ)UC(\mathbb{H})\subseteq S(\mathbb{H}). Furthermore,‖X​(z)‖<1\|X(z)\|<1sinceρ1,ρ2\rho_{1},\rho_{2}are unital∗*-representations and‖E​(z)‖∞,𝔻¯2,‖e​(z)‖∞,𝔻¯<1\|E(z)\|_{\infty,\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}},\|e(z)\|_{\infty,\overline{\mathbb{D}}}<1for allz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H}.

Given a compact setK⊆ℂnK\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}, a unital∗*-representationρ:C​(K)→ℬ​(ℋ)\rho:C(K)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})is said to besimpleif there existz1,…,zm∈Kz_{1},\dotsc,z_{m}\in Kand orthogonal projectionsP1,…,Pm∈ℬ​(ℋ)P_{1},\dotsc,P_{m}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})withP1+…+Pm=IℋP_{1}+\dotsc+P_{m}=I_{\mathcal{H}}such thatρ​(f)=f​(z1)​P1+…+f​(zm)​Pm\rho(f)=f(z_{1})P_{1}+\dotsc+f(z_{m})P_{m}for allf∈C​(K)f\in C(K).

## Theorem 2.7.

Letf:ℍ→ℂf:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}be a function. Then the following are equivalent:
- (1)(1)

f∈S​A​(ℍ)f\in SA(\mathbb{H});
- (2)(2)

- (3)(3)

there existξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)ℍ+,∇∈C​(𝔻¯)ℍ+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}},\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}}andδ∈ℂℍ+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{H}}^{+}such that for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},1−f​(z)​f​(w)¯=ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w),1-f(z)\overline{f(w)}=\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w),

whereE​(z)E(z)ande​(z)e(z)are as in (2);
- (4)(4)

f∈U​C​(ℍ)f\in UC(\mathbb{H}).

## Proof.

We have divided the proof into several steps for a clear understanding of the readers.

(1)⟹(2)(1)\implies(2). Letk∈A​K​(ℍ)k\in AK(\mathbb{H}). We first prove this implication for functions inS​A​(ℍ)∩Hol​(ℍ¯)SA(\mathbb{H})\cap\text{Hol}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}). We then show that every function inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})can be approximated by a sequence of functions fromS​A​(ℍ)∩Hol​(ℍ¯)SA(\mathbb{H})\cap\text{Hol}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}), which gives the desired conclusion. It is not difficult to see that eachMz(j)M_{z^{(j)}}is bounded, and(2.4)‖Mz(3)‖≤1,‖Ψ​(α,Mz(2),Mz(3),Mz(4))‖≤1and‖ψα1,α2​(Mz(1),Mz(2),Mz(3),Mz(4))‖≤1\|M_{z^{(3)}}\|\leq 1,\quad\|\Psi(\alpha,M_{z^{(2)}},M_{z^{(3)}},M_{z^{(4)}})\|\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(M_{z^{(1)}},M_{z^{(2)}},M_{z^{(3)}},M_{z^{(4)}})\|\leq 1

for allα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}. Let{z1,…,zm}⊆ℍ\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{m}\}\subseteq\mathbb{H}and letℋm​(k)\mathcal{H}_{m}(k)be the finite dimensional Hilbert space spanned by{k(.,z1),…,k(.,zm)}\{k(.,z_{1}),\dotsc,k(.,z_{m})\}. disjoint from it, there is a continuous bounded function separating the two). Consequently,𝔻¯ℍ\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{\mathbb{H}}is Tychonov and so,Λ\Lambdais a Tychonov space. ## Interpolation theorem on the hexablock.

Given{z1,…,zn}⊆ℍ\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{H}andλ1,…,λn∈𝔻¯\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}, the interpolation problem associated withS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})asks for necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of a functionf∈S​A​(ℍ)f\in SA(\mathbb{H})that maps eachziz_{i}toλi\lambda_{i}. As an application of Theorem2.7, various characterizations of such an interpolation problem forℍ\mathbb{H}are presented below.

- (1)(1)

there exists a functionf∈S​A​(ℍ)f\in SA(\mathbb{H})such thatf​(zi)=λif(z_{i})=\lambda_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n;

- (3)(3)

there existξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)F+,∇∈C​(𝔻¯)F+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{F},\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{F}andδ∈ℂF+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{F}^{+}such that for alli,j∈{1,…,n}i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,n\},1−λi​λ¯j=ξ​(zi,zj)​(1−E​(zi)​E​(zj)¯)+∇(zi,zj)​(1−e​(zi)​e​(zj)¯)+(1−zi(3)​z¯j(3))​δ​(zi,zj).1-\lambda_{i}\overline{\lambda}_{j}=\xi(z_{i},z_{j})(1-E(z_{i})\overline{E(z_{j})})+\nabla(z_{i},z_{j})(1-e(z_{i})\overline{e(z_{j})})+(1-z_{i}^{(3)}\overline{z}_{j}^{(3)})\delta(z_{i},z_{j}).

## Extension theorem on the hexablock

LetWWbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}. Denote byℋ​ℰ​(W)\mathscr{HE}(W)the collection of all bounded functions onWWthat admits an extension to a holomorphic function in a neighbourhood ofWW. In this section, we study the following extension problem: given a subsetW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}, determine necessary and sufficient conditions under which a functionf∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W)admits a norm-preserving extension to a functiong∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})such thatg/‖f‖∞,W∈S​A​(ℍ)g/\penalty 50\|f\|_{\infty,W}\in SA(\mathbb{H}). To do so, we introduce some terminologies and definitions. Recall from (2.2) thatz=(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℍz=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{H}if and only if|z(3)|<1,|Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))|<1|z^{(3)}|<1,|\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})|<1and|ψα1,α2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))|<1|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})|<1for allα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}, whereΨ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))=α​z(4)−z(2)α​z(3)−1andψα1,α2​(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))=z(1)​(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)1−z(2)​α1−z(3)​α2+z(4)​α1​α2.\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=\frac{\alpha z^{(4)}-z^{(2)}}{\alpha z^{(3)}-1}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})=\frac{z^{(1)}\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}}{1-z^{(2)}\alpha_{1}-z^{(3)}\alpha_{2}+z^{(4)}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}.

LetQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}be the class of all commuting quadruplesT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})of Hilbert space operators withσT​(T¯)⊆ℍ\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})\subseteq\mathbb{H}such that
for allα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}and(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2},‖T3‖≤1,‖Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4)‖≤1and‖ψα1,α2​(T1,T2,T3,T4)‖≤1,\|T_{3}\|\leq 1,\quad\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|\leq 1,

whereΨ​(α,T2,T3,T4)=(α​T4−T2)​(α​T3−1)−1\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})=(\alpha T_{4}-T_{2})(\alpha T_{3}-1)^{-1}andψα1,α2​(T1,T2,T3,T4)=(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)​T1​(I−α1​T2−α2​T3+α1​α2​T4)−1.\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})=\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}T_{1}(I-\alpha_{1}T_{2}-\alpha_{2}T_{3}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}T_{4})^{-1}.

Following the terminology in[42]for quantized domains inℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}, we refer the classQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}as thequantum hexablock. By quantization, we mean the process of replacing scalars with operator variables subject to analogous norm constraints. Recall from Section2that𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}is the set of all commuting quadruplesT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})of Hilbert space operators such that
for allα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}},‖T3‖<1,‖Ψ​(α,T2,T3,T4)‖<1and‖ψα1,α2​(T1,T2,T3,T4)‖<1.\|T_{3}\|<1,\quad\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1\quad\text{and}\quad\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1.

It was proved in the beginning of Section2thatσT​(T¯)⊆ℍ\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})\subseteq\mathbb{H}for allT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}. Consequently, it follows that the class𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}is contained in the quantum pentablockQ​ℍQ\mathbb{H}.

ForW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}, we say that a commuting quadrupleT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})issubordinatetoWWifσT​(T¯)⊂W\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})\subset Wandg​(T¯)=0g(\underline{T})=0wheneverggis holomorphic in a neighbourhood ofWWandg|W=0g|_{W}=0. The definition off​(T¯)f(\underline{T})is independent of the choice of the holomorphic extensionggofff. To see this, lethhbe any other holomorphic extension offfin some neighbourhood ofWW. Clearly, the functiong−hg-his holomorphic on a neighbourhood ofWWandg−h=0g-h=0onWW. SinceT¯\underline{T}is subordinate toWW, we have thatg​(T¯)=h​(T¯)g(\underline{T})=h(\underline{T}). Having introduced the required definitions, we first present the statement of the main theorem in this section.

## Theorem 2.9.

LetW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}and letf∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W)be non-zero. Then there existsg∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})such that1‖f‖∞,W​g∈S​A​(ℍ),g|W=f\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty,W}}g\in SA(\mathbb{H}),g|_{W}=fand‖g‖∞,ℍ=‖f‖∞,W\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}=\|f\|_{\infty,W}if and only if‖f​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,Wfor everyT¯∈Q​ℍsubordinate toW.\|f(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}\quad\text{for every $\underline{T}\in Q\mathbb{H}$ subordinate to $W$}.

To prove Theorem2.9, we adopt an approach based on the interpolation theorem, in the spirit of[7],[24]and[39]for the bidisc, symmetrized bidisc and tetrablock, respectively. A first step in this direction is the following lemma which establishes the necessary condition of Theorem2.9.

## Lemma 2.10.

LetW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}and letf∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W)be non-zero. If there existsg∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})such that1‖f‖∞,W​g∈S​A​(ℍ),g|W=f\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty,W}}g\in SA(\mathbb{H}),g|_{W}=fand‖g‖∞,ℍ=‖f‖∞,W\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}=\|f\|_{\infty,W}, then‖f​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,W\|f(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for everyT¯∈Q​ℍ\underline{T}\in Q\mathbb{H}subordinate toWW.

## Proof.

LetT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)∈Q​ℍ\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in Q\mathbb{H}be subordinate toWW, and letT¯\underline{T}be acting on a Hilbert space𝒦\mathcal{K}. Setr⋅z=(r​z(1),r​z(2),r​z(3),r2​z(4))r\cdot z=(rz^{(1)},rz^{(2)},rz^{(3)},r^{2}z^{(4)})forz=(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))∈ℂ4z=(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\in\mathbb{C}^{4}and0<r<10<r<1. Definegn:ℍ→ℂg_{n}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}asgn​(z)=g​(rn⋅z)g_{n}(z)=g(r_{n}\cdot z). Then{gn}\{g_{n}\}is a uniformly bounded sequence of holomorphic functions converging pointwise togg. An application of the dominated convergence theorem gives thatgn​(T¯)g_{n}(\underline{T})converges tog​(T¯)g(\underline{T})in the weak operator topology. Sincern⋅T¯∈𝔐ℍr_{n}\cdot\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}and1‖f‖∞,W​g∈S​A​(ℍ)\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty,W}}g\in SA(\mathbb{H}), we have that|⟨g​(T¯)​x,y⟩|=limn→∞|⟨g​(rn⋅T¯)​x,y⟩|≤limn→∞‖g​(rn⋅T¯)‖​‖x‖​‖y‖≤‖f‖∞,W​‖x‖​‖y‖.|\langle g(\underline{T})x,y\rangle|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\langle g(r_{n}\cdot\underline{T})x,y\rangle|\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|g(r_{n}\cdot\underline{T})\|\|x\|\|y\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}\|x\|\|y\|.Thus,‖f​(T¯)‖=‖g​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,W\|f(\underline{T})\|=\|g(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}.

The proof of the converse to Theorem2.9brings out a subtle relationship between classical extremal problems and operator theoretic techniques. We first reformulate the interpolation theorem forℍ\mathbb{H}in a form suited to the proof of the extension theorem. Letz1=(z1(1),z1(2),z1(3),z1(4)),…,zn=(zn(1),zn(2),zn(3),zn(4))z_{1}=(z_{1}^{(1)},z_{1}^{(2)},z_{1}^{(3)},z_{1}^{(4)}),\dotsc,z_{n}=(z_{n}^{(1)},z_{n}^{(2)},z_{n}^{(3)},z_{n}^{(4)})be distinct points inℍ\mathbb{H}. We denote this data byz. &\left[\left(1-\Psi(\alpha,z_{i}^{(2)},z_{i}^{(3)},z_{i}^{(4)})\overline{\Psi(\alpha,z_{j}^{(2)},z_{j}^{(3)},z_{j}^{(4)})}\right)k(i,j)\right]_{i,j=1}^{n}\geq 0\quad\text{for all}\ \alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}},\\[5.0pt]
&\left[\left(1-\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z_{i})\overline{\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(z_{j})}\right)k(i,j)\right]\geq 0\quad\text{for all}\ (\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right\}

It follows from (2.14) thatk​(M1​v)=k​(M2​v)=k​(M3​v)=k​(M4​v)=0k(M_{1}v)=k(M_{2}v)=k(M_{3}v)=k(M_{4}v)=0and so,k​(p​(M1,M2,M3,M4)​v)=0k(p(M_{1},M_{2},M_{3},M_{4})v)=0for any holomorphic polynomialppin four variables. Thenk​(p​(M1,M2,M3,M4)​v)k(p(M_{1},M_{2},M_{3},M_{4})v)equalsviv_{i}times the(i,i)(i,i)-th column ofkkand thusk​(i,i)=0k(i,i)=0, which gives a contradiction ask​(i,i)=1k(i,i)=1. ## Theorem 2.11.

Letz1,…,znz_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}be distinct points inℍ\mathbb{H}, and letλ1,…,λn∈𝔻¯\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}. which is clearly nonempty. Evidently,‖g‖∞,ℍ≤‖g‖𝔐ℍ≤‖f‖∞,W\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}\leq\|g\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for allg∈S​Af​(ℍ)g\in SA_{f}(\mathbb{H}). For the given dataz={z1,…,zn}⊆W\textbf{z}=\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subseteq Wand𝝀=(λ1,…,λn)∈ℂn\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}, we denote byρf(z,𝝀)=inf{∥g∥𝔐ℍ:g∈SAf(ℍ)andg(zi)=λi,1≤i≤n}.\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda})=\inf\left\{\|g\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}:g\in SA_{f}(\mathbb{H})\ \text{and}\ g(z_{i})=\lambda_{i},1\leq i\leq n\right\}.

Such aggis called anextremal functionfor the datazand𝝀\bm{\lambda}.

## Lemma 2.12.

LetWWbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}and letf∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W)be non-zero. For givenz={z1,…,zn}⊆W\textbf{z}=\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subseteq Wand𝛌=(λ1,…,λn)∈ℂn\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}, there exists an extremal function inS​Af​(ℍ)SA_{f}(\mathbb{H})for the datazand𝛌\bm{\lambda}.

## Proof.

Let{gm}\{g_{m}\}be a sequence inS​Af​(ℍ)SA_{f}(\mathbb{H})such thatgm​(zi)=λig_{m}(z_{i})=\lambda_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq nandρf​(z,𝝀)=limm→∞​‖gm‖𝔐ℍ\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda})=\underset{m\to\infty}{\lim}\|g_{m}\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}. Evidently,‖g‖∞,ℍ≤‖g‖𝔐ℍ≤‖f‖∞,W\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}\leq\|g\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for everyg∈S​Af​(ℍ)g\in SA_{f}(\mathbb{H}). Therefore, one can find a subsequence{gmk}\{g_{m_{k}}\}of{gm}\{g_{m}\}which converges pointwise tog∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}). For everyT¯\underline{T}in𝔐ℍ\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}, an application of dominated convergence theorem gives thatgmk​(T¯)g_{m_{k}}(\underline{T})converges tog​(T¯)g(\underline{T})in the weak-operator topology. Consequently,‖g​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,W\|g(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for allT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}andg​(zi)=λig(z_{i})=\lambda_{i}for1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq n. By definition ofρf​(z,𝝀)\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda}), it follows thatρf​(z,𝝀)≤‖g‖𝔐ℍ\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda})\leq\|g\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}. LetT¯\underline{T}be a commuting quadruple of operators acting on a Hilbert space𝒦\mathcal{K}, and letT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}. Then
for everyx,y∈𝒦x,y\in\mathcal{K},|⟨g​(T¯)​x,y⟩|=limk→∞|⟨gmk​(T¯)​x,y⟩|≤limk→∞‖gmk​(T¯)‖​‖x‖​‖y‖≤limk→∞‖gmk‖𝔐ℍ​‖x‖​‖y‖=ρf​(z,𝝀)​‖x‖​‖y‖.|\langle g(\underline{T})x,y\rangle|=\lim_{k\to\infty}|\langle g_{m_{k}}(\underline{T})x,y\rangle|\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\|g_{m_{k}}(\underline{T})\|\|x\|\|y\|\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\|g_{m_{k}}\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}\|x\|\|y\|=\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda})\|x\|\|y\|.

Therefore,‖g​(T¯)‖≤ρf​(z,𝝀)\|g(\underline{T})\|\leq\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda})for allT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}and so,‖g‖𝔐ℍ≤ρf​(z,𝝀)\|g\|_{\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}}\leq\rho_{f}(\textbf{z},\bm{\lambda}). The proof is complete.

ForW⊆ℍW\subseteq\mathbb{H}, we say thatWWhas theextension propertyinS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})if for every non-zerof∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W), there existsg∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})such that1‖f‖∞,W​g∈S​A​(ℍ),g|W=f\frac{1}{\|f\|_{\infty,W}}g\in SA(\mathbb{H}),g|_{W}=fand‖f‖∞,W=‖g‖∞,ℍ\|f\|_{\infty,W}=\|g\|_{\infty,\mathbb{H}}. ## Corollary 2.14.

A subsetWWofℍ\mathbb{H}has the extension property inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})if and only if‖f​(T¯)‖≤‖f‖∞,W\|f(\underline{T})\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty,W}for everyf∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W)andT¯∈Q​ℍ\underline{T}\in Q\mathbb{H}subordinate toWW.

## Toeplitz corona theorem on the hexablock

One of the key results in this article is the realization theorem for functions in the Schur-Agler classS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})forℍ\mathbb{H}. To do so, the notion of admissible kernels associated withℍ\mathbb{H}is introduced. In this direction, we present the vector-valued analog of admissible kernels onℍ\mathbb{H}. Letℒ\mathcal{L}be a Hilbert space, and letFFbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}. Aℬ​(ℒ)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L})-valued weak kernel ofFFis a positive semi-definite functionk:F×F→ℬ​(ℒ)k:F\times F\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}), that is,∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨k​(zi,zj)​vj,vi⟩ℒ≥0\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})v_{j},v_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}\geq 0

for every finite set{z1,…,zn}⊂F\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subset Fand vectorsv1,…,vn∈ℒv_{1},\dotsc,v_{n}\in\mathcal{L}. In addition, ifk​(z,z)≠0k(z,z)\neq 0for allz∈Fz\in F, we say thatkkis aℬ​(ℒ)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L})-valued kernel. Aℬ​(ℒ)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L})-valued kernelkkis said to beadmissibleif
- (1)

(z,w)↦(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​k​(z,w)(z,w)\mapsto\left(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)}\right)k(z,w)
- (2)

(z,w)↦(1−Ψ​(α,z(2),z(3),z(4))​Ψ​(α,w(2),w(3),w(4))¯)​k​(z,w)(z,w)\mapsto\left(1-\Psi(\alpha,z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\overline{\Psi(\alpha,w^{(2)},w^{(3)},w^{(4)})}\right)k(z,w)
- (3)

are positive semi-definite maps for allα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}and(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}. The class of scalar-valued admissible kernels onℍ\mathbb{H}is denoted byA​K​(ℍ)AK(\mathbb{H}). For a compact setK⊆ℂnK\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}and a subsetFFofℍ\mathbb{H}, a mapξ:F×F→ℬ​(C​(K),ℬ​(ℒ))\xi:F\times F\to\mathcal{B}(C(K),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}))is calledcompletely positive kernelif for everyn∈ℕ,{v1,…,vn}⊂ℒ,{z1,…,zn}⊂Fn\in\mathbb{N},\{v_{1},\dotsc,v_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{L},\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subset Fand{h1,…,hn}⊂C​(K)\{h_{1},\dotsc,h_{n}\}\subset C(K), we have∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨ξ​(zi,zj)​(hi​h¯j)​vj,vi⟩ℒ≥0.\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\langle\xi(z_{i},z_{j})(h_{i}\overline{h}_{j})v_{j},v_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}}\geq 0.

Ifℒ=ℂ\mathcal{L}=\mathbb{C}, thenℬ​(C​(K),ℒ)\mathcal{B}(C(K),\mathcal{L})is the dual spaceC​(K)∗C(K)^{*}ofC​(K)C(K). In this case, a completely positive kernelξ:F×F→C​(K)∗\xi:F\times F\to C(K)^{*}is simply called apositive kernel. To prove Toeplitz corona theorem onℍ\mathbb{H}, we require an operator-valued analogue of the realization theorem onℍ\mathbb{H}. Since its proof can be simply obtained from the scalar-valued case by the necessary modifications, we shall state the result without proof. We mention that the proof of the scalar-valued realization theorem onℍ\mathbb{H}. Accordingly, we first establish vector-valued analogues of these two results. As a basic step, we state the following result and its proof follows the same argument as that of Proposition2.2.

## Proposition 2.15.

LetFFbe a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}and letKKbe a compact subset ofℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}. for allf,g∈C​(K)f,g\in C(K)andz,w∈Fz,w\in F.

Letk1,k2k_{1},k_{2}beℬ​(ℒ)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L})-valued functions onℍ×ℍ\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}. Following the notations in Definition 11.25 of[6], we setk1⊘k2:ℍ×ℍ→ℬ​(ℒ⊗ℒ)k_{1}\oslash k_{2}:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L})as(k1⊘k2)​(z,w)=k1​(z,w)⊗k2​(z,w)(k_{1}\oslash k_{2})(z,w)=k_{1}(z,w)\otimes k_{2}(z,w)forz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H}. We skip its proof as it is a routine adaptation of the scalar-valued arguments presented in Theorem2.3.

## Theorem 2.16.

for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H}.

Before stating the operator-valued realization theorem forℍ\mathbb{H}, we introduce a vector-valued analogue of the Schur-Agler classS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H}). For a domainΩ\Omegainℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}and Hilbert spacesℒ1,ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}, letg:Ω→ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)g:\Omega\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})be an analytic function. SupposeT¯=(T1,…,Td)\underline{T}=(T_{1},\dotsc,T_{d})is a commuting tuple of operators on a Hilbert spaceℒ\mathcal{L}withσT​(T¯)⊆Ω\sigma_{T}(\underline{T})\subseteq\Omega. Given an open neighbourhoodU0U_{0}ofσT​(T¯)\sigma_{T}(\underline{T}), there is a unique continuous linear mapg↦g​(T¯)g\mapsto g(\underline{T})fromHol​(U0,ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2))≡Hol​(U0)⊗ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)\text{Hol}(U_{0},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}))\equiv\text{Hol}(U_{0})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})intoℬ​(ℒ⊗ℒ1,ℒ⊗ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2})takingg⊗Bg\otimes Bintog​(T¯)⊗Bg(\underline{T})\otimes Bforg∈Hol​(U0)g\in\text{Hol}(U_{0})andB∈ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}). Therefore, it follows that⟨g​(T¯)​(h1⊗x),h2⊗y⟩=⟨gx,y​(T¯)​h1,h2⟩\langle g(\underline{T})(h_{1}\otimes x),h_{2}\otimes y\rangle=\langle g_{x,y}(\underline{T})h_{1},h_{2}\rangle

for everyh1,h2∈ℒ,x∈ℒ1h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{L},x\in\mathcal{L}_{1}andy∈ℒ2y\in\mathcal{L}_{2}, wheregx,y:Ω→ℂg_{x,y}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}is the scalar-valued holomorphic map defined bygx,y​(z)=⟨g​(z)​x,y⟩g_{x,y}(z)=\langle g(z)x,y\rangle. An interested reader is referred to[14,36]for further details.

For Hilbert spacesℒ1,ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}, theℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})-valued Schur-Agler classS​Aℍ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})is the collection of all holomorphic functionsg:ℍ→ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)g:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})satisfying‖g​(T¯)‖≤1\|g(\underline{T})\|\leq 1for everyT¯∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}. The following theorem extends the realization theorem established in Theorem2.7to the operator-valued setting. It characterizes the classS​Aℍ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})in terms of a realization formula. Since the proof is a routine adaptation of that of Theorem2.7, we omit the details.

0&0&z^{(3)}I_{\mathcal{H}_{3}}\end{bmatrix}\left(I-D\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}(E(z))&0&0\\
0&\rho_{2}(e(z))&0\\
0&0&z^{(3)}I_{\mathcal{H}_{3}}\end{bmatrix}\right)^{-1}C.

We are now in a position to establish a vector-valued version of the Toeplitz corona theorem forℍ\mathbb{H}. For givenφ1,…,φd∈H∞​(ℍ)\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{d}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}), Toeplitz corona theorem characterizes the existence of functionsf1,…,fd∈H∞​(ℍ)f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})such thatφ1​f1+…+φd​fd=1\varphi_{1}f_{1}+\dotsc+\varphi_{d}f_{d}=1withf1,…,fdf_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}subjected to a certain norm bound condition. The vector-valued formulation provides a natural extension of this result and the scalar-valued theorem follows as an immediate consequence.

## Theorem 2.18.

Letℒ1,ℒ2\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}andℒ3\mathcal{L}_{3}be Hilbert spaces. For functionsf12:ℍ→ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ2)f_{12}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2})andf32:ℍ→ℬ​(ℒ3,ℒ2)f_{32}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{2}), the following are equivalent:
- (1)(1)

there existsf31∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ3,ℒ1)f_{31}\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{1})such thatf12​(z)​f31​(z)=f32​(z)f_{12}(z)f_{31}(z)=f_{32}(z)for everyz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H};
- (2)(2)

for every finite subsetF={z1,…,zn}⊂ℍF=\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{H},[(f12​(zi)​f12​(zj)∗−f32​(zi)​f32​(zj)∗)⊗k​(zi,zj)]i,j=1n≥0\begin{bmatrix}\left(f_{12}(z_{i})f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}-f_{32}(z_{i})f_{32}(z_{j})^{*}\right)\otimes k(z_{i},z_{j})\end{bmatrix}_{i,j=1}^{n}\geq 0

for everyℬ​(ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})-valued admissible kernelkkonℍ\mathbb{H};
- (3)(3)

there exist a weakℬ​(ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})-valued kernelδ\deltaonℍ\mathbb{H}and completely positive kernelsξ:ℍ×ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯2),ℬ​(ℒ2)),∇:ℍ×ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯),ℬ​(ℒ2))\xi:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})),\nabla:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2}))such that for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},f12​(z)​f12​(w)∗−f32​(z)​f32​(w)∗\displaystyle f_{12}(z)f_{12}(w)^{*}-f_{32}(z)f_{32}(w)^{*}=ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w),\displaystyle=\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w),

where the mapsz↦E​(z)z\mapsto E(z)andz↦e​(z)z\mapsto e(z)are as in (2).

## Proof.

The implication(2)⟹(3)(2)\implies(3)follows directly from Theorem2.16applied to the self-adjoint function(z,w)↦f12​(z)​f12​(w)∗−f32​(z)​f32​(w)∗(z,w)\mapsto f_{12}(z)f_{12}(w)^{*}-f_{32}(z)f_{32}(w)^{*}. We now prove(1)⟹(2)(1)\implies(2)and(3)⟹(1)(3)\implies(1).

(1)⟹(2)(1)\implies(2). LetF={z1,…,zn}F=\{z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n}\}be a subset ofℍ\mathbb{H}and letkkbe aℬ​(ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})-valued admissible kernel onℍ\mathbb{H}. Forw∈ℍw\in\mathbb{H}andh∈ℒ2h\in\mathcal{L}_{2}, we definekw​h:ℍ→ℒ2k_{w}h:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{L}_{2}askw​h​(z)=k​(z,w)​hk_{w}h(z)=k(z,w)h. Letℋ​(k)=span¯​{kz​h:z∈ℍ,h∈ℒ2}andℋn​(k)=span¯​{kzi​h:h∈ℒ2,1≤i≤n}.\mathcal{H}(k)=\overline{\text{span}}\left\{k_{z}h:z\in\mathbb{H},h\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\right\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathcal{H}_{n}(k)=\overline{\text{span}}\{k_{z_{i}}h:h\in\mathcal{L}_{2},1\leq i\leq n\}.

Clearly,ℋ​(k)\mathcal{H}(k)is the reproducing kernel Hilbert space associated with the operator-valued kernelkk, and is equipped with the inner product⟨kz​h,kw​h′⟩ℋ​(k)=⟨k​(w,z)​h,h′⟩ℒ2\langle k_{z}h,k_{w}h^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(k)}=\langle k(w,z)h,h^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}. Consider the commuting quadrupleM¯=(Mz(1),Mz(2),Mz(3),Mz(4))\underline{M}=(M_{z^{(1)}},M_{z^{(2)}},M_{z^{(3)}},M_{z^{(4)}})consisting of the coordinate multiplication operators onℋ​(k)\mathcal{H}(k). Note thatMz(i)∗​(kz​h)=kz​(z¯(i)​h)M_{z^{(i)}}^{*}(k_{z}h)=k_{z}(\overline{z}^{(i)}h)for1≤i≤41\leq i\leq 4. For everyα∈𝔻¯\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}and(α1,α2)∈𝔻¯2(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}.(2.17)‖Mz(3)‖≤1,‖Ψ​(α,Mz(2),Mz(3),Mz(4))‖≤1and‖ψα1,α2​(Mz(1),Mz(2),Mz(3),Mz(4))‖≤1\displaystyle\|M_{z^{(3)}}\|\leq 1,\ \ \|\Psi(\alpha,M_{z^{(2)}},M_{z^{(3)}},M_{z^{(4)}})\|\leq 1\ \ \text{and}\ \ \|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(M_{z^{(1)}},M_{z^{(2)}},M_{z^{(3)}},M_{z^{(4)}})\|\leq 1

Also,ℋn​(k)\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)is a joint invariant subspace forM¯∗=(Mz(1)∗,Mz(2)∗,Mz(3)∗,Mz(4)∗)\underline{M}^{*}=(M_{z^{(1)}}^{*},M_{z^{(2)}}^{*},M_{z^{(3)}}^{*},M_{z^{(4)}}^{*})sinceMz(i)∗​(kzj​h)=kzj​(z¯j(i)​h)M_{z^{(i)}}^{*}(k_{z_{j}}h)=k_{z_{j}}(\overline{z}_{j}^{(i)}h)for1≤i≤41\leq i\leq 4and1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n. ConsiderT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})onℋn​(k)\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)given by(T1∗,T2∗,T3∗,T4∗)=(Mz(1)∗|ℋn​(k),Mz(2)∗|ℋn​(k),Mz(3)∗|ℋn​(k),Mz(4)∗|ℋn​(k)).(T_{1}^{*},T_{2}^{*},T_{3}^{*},T_{4}^{*})=\left(M_{z^{(1)}}^{*}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)},M_{z^{(2)}}^{*}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)},M_{z^{(3)}}^{*}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)},M_{z^{(4)}}^{*}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)}\right).

Sinceℍ\mathbb{H}is a(1,1,1,2)(1,1,1,2)-quasi-balanced domain, it follows from (2.17) that the quadruples⋅T¯∗=(s​T1∗,s​T2∗,s​T3∗,s2​T4∗)∈𝔐ℍs\cdot\underline{T}^{*}=(sT_{1}^{*},sT_{2}^{*},sT_{3}^{*},s^{2}T_{4}^{*})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}for everys∈(0,1)s\in(0,1). Take a holomorphic mapf:ℍ¯→ℬ​(ℒ3,ℒ1)f:\overline{\mathbb{H}}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{1})such thatf∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ3,ℒ1)f\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{1})and definef^:ℍ¯→ℬ​(ℒ1,ℒ3)\widehat{f}:\overline{\mathbb{H}}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})asf^​(z)=f​(z¯)∗\widehat{f}(z)=f(\overline{z})^{*}forz∈ℍ¯z\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}. Thenf^∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ1,ℒ3)\widehat{f}\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3}). Evidently,f^​(s⋅T¯∗)∈ℬ​(ℋn​(k)⊗ℒ1,ℋn​(k)⊗ℒ3)\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{3})that satisfies(2.18)f^​(s⋅T¯∗)​(kzj​u⊗v)=kzj​u⊗f​(s⋅zj)∗​v\displaystyle\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})(k_{z_{j}}u\otimes v)=k_{z_{j}}u\otimes f(s\cdot z_{j})^{*}v

for everyu∈ℒ2,v∈ℒ1u\in\mathcal{L}_{2},v\in\mathcal{L}_{1}and1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n. To see this, note that⟨f^​(s⋅T¯∗)​(kzj​uj⊗vj),kzi​ui⊗vi⟩\displaystyle\langle\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})(k_{z_{j}}u_{j}\otimes v_{j}),k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\otimes v_{i}\rangle=⟨f^vj,vi​(s⋅T¯∗)​kzj​uj,kzi​ui⟩\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{f}_{v_{j},v_{i}}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})k_{z_{j}}u_{j},k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\rangle=⟨kzj​(f^vj,vi​(s⋅z¯j)​uj),kzi​ui⟩[Mz(i)∗​(kzj​h)=kzj​(z¯j(i)​h)​for​1≤i≤4]\displaystyle=\langle k_{z_{j}}(\widehat{f}_{v_{j},v_{i}}(s\cdot\overline{z}_{j})u_{j}),k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\rangle\quad[M_{z^{(i)}}^{*}(k_{z_{j}}h)=k_{z_{j}}(\overline{z}_{j}^{(i)}h)\ \text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq 4]=⟨k​(zi,zj)​(f^vj,vi​(s⋅z¯j)​uj),ui⟩\displaystyle=\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})(\widehat{f}_{v_{j},v_{i}}(s\cdot\overline{z}_{j})u_{j}),u_{i}\rangle=f^vj,vi​(s⋅z¯j)​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩\displaystyle=\widehat{f}_{v_{j},v_{i}}(s\cdot\overline{z}_{j})\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\rangle=⟨f^​(s⋅z¯j)​vj,vi⟩​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{f}(s\cdot\overline{z}_{j})v_{j},v_{i}\rangle\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\rangle=⟨f​(s⋅zj)∗​vj,vi⟩​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩\displaystyle=\langle f(s\cdot z_{j})^{*}v_{j},v_{i}\rangle\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\rangle=⟨kzj​uj⊗f​(s⋅z¯j)∗​vj,kzi​ui⊗vi⟩\displaystyle=\langle k_{z_{j}}u_{j}\otimes f(s\cdot\overline{z}_{j})^{*}v_{j},k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\otimes v_{i}\rangle

for everyui,uj∈ℒ2,vi∈ℒ3u_{i},u_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{2},v_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{3}andvj∈ℒ1v_{j}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}with1≤i,j≤n1\leq i,j\leq n.
Let{ui,vi:1≤i≤n}⊂ℒ2\{u_{i},v_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}\subset\mathcal{L}_{2}. Sincef^∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ1,ℒ3)\widehat{f}\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3})ands⋅T¯∗∈𝔐ℍs\cdot\underline{T}^{*}\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}, it follows that‖f^​(s⋅T¯∗)‖≤1\|\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})\|\leq 1. Then0\displaystyle 0≤⟨(Iℋn​(k)⊗ℋ1−f^​(s⋅T¯∗)∗​f^​(s⋅T¯∗))​[∑j=1𝑛​kzj​uj⊗f12​(zj)∗​vj],[∑i=1𝑛​kzi​ui⊗f12​(zi)∗​vi]⟩\displaystyle\leq\left\langle\left(I_{\mathcal{H}_{n}(k)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{1}}-\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})^{*}\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})\right)\left[\overset{n}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}k_{z_{j}}u_{j}\otimes f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}v_{j}\right],\left[\overset{n}{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\otimes f_{12}(z_{i})^{*}v_{i}\right]\right\rangle=∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨kzj​uj⊗f12​(zj)∗​vj,kzi​ui⊗f12​(zi)∗​vi⟩\displaystyle=\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\left\langle k_{z_{j}}u_{j}\otimes f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}v_{j},k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\otimes f_{12}(z_{i})^{*}v_{i}\right\rangle−∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨f^​(s⋅T¯∗)​[kzj​uj⊗f12​(zj)∗​vj],f^​(s⋅T¯∗)​[kzi​ui⊗f12​(zi)∗​vi]⟩\displaystyle\quad-\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\left\langle\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})\left[k_{z_{j}}u_{j}\otimes f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}v_{j}\right],\widehat{f}(s\cdot\underline{T}^{*})\left[k_{z_{i}}u_{i}\otimes f_{12}(z_{i})^{*}v_{i}\right]\right\rangle=∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩​⟨(f12​(zi)​f12​(zj)∗−f12​(zi)​f​(s⋅zi)​f​(s⋅zj)∗​f12​(zj)∗)​vj,vi⟩\displaystyle=\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\left\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle\left(f_{12}(z_{i})f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}-f_{12}(z_{i})f(s\cdot z_{i})f(s\cdot z_{j})^{*}f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}\right)v_{j},v_{i}\right\rangle

and taking the limits→1−s\to 1^{-}, it follows that(2.19)∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩​⟨(f12​(zi)​f12​(zj)∗−f12​(zi)​f​(zi)​f​(zj)∗​f12​(zj)∗)​vj,vi⟩≥0.\displaystyle\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\left\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle\left(f_{12}(z_{i})f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}-f_{12}(z_{i})f(z_{i})f(z_{j})^{*}f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}\right)v_{j},v_{i}\right\rangle\geq 0.

Forf31∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ3,ℒ1)f_{31}\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{1}), apply the above argument to the functionsf31(r)​(z)=f31​(r⋅z)f_{31}^{(r)}(z)=f_{31}(r\cdot z), where0<r<10<r<1. Lettingr→1−r\to 1^{-}gives that∑i,j=1𝑛​⟨k​(zi,zj)​uj,ui⟩​⟨(f12​(zi)​f12​(zj)∗−f12​(zi)​f31​(zi)​f31​(zj)∗​f12​(zj)∗)​vj,vi⟩≥0.\displaystyle\overset{n}{\underset{i,j=1}{\sum}}\left\langle k(z_{i},z_{j})u_{j},u_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle\left(f_{12}(z_{i})f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}-f_{12}(z_{i})f_{31}(z_{i})f_{31}(z_{j})^{*}f_{12}(z_{j})^{*}\right)v_{j},v_{i}\right\rangle\geq 0.

(3)⟹(1)(3)\implies(1). Assume that there exist a weakℬ​(ℒ2)\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})-valued kernelδ\deltaonℍ\mathbb{H}and completely positive kernelsξ:ℍ×ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯2),ℬ​(ℒ2)),∇:ℍ×ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯),ℬ​(ℒ2))\xi:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2})),\nabla:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{2}))such that for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},f12​(z)​f12​(w)∗−f32​(z)​f32​(w)∗\displaystyle f_{12}(z)f_{12}(w)^{*}-f_{32}(z)f_{32}(w)^{*}(2.20)=ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w).\displaystyle=\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w).

By Proposition2.15, there exist Hilbert space𝒦1,𝒦2\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}and mapsL1:ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯2),ℬ​(𝒦1,ℒ2))L_{1}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}))andL2:ℍ→ℬ​(C​(𝔻¯),ℬ​(𝒦2,ℒ2))L_{2}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{2}))such that for everyz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},f1,h1∈C​(𝔻¯2)f_{1},h_{1}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})andf2,h2∈C​(𝔻¯)f_{2},h_{2}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}),(2.21)ξ​(z,w)​(f1​h¯1)=L1​(z)​(f1)​(L1​(w)​(h1))∗and∇(z,w)​(f2​h¯2)=L2​(z)​(f2)​(L2​(w)​(h2))∗.\displaystyle\xi(z,w)(f_{1}\overline{h}_{1})=L_{1}(z)(f_{1})(L_{1}(w)(h_{1}))^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla(z,w)(f_{2}\overline{h}_{2})=L_{2}(z)(f_{2})(L_{2}(w)(h_{2}))^{*}.

We also have unital∗*-representationsρ1:C​(𝔻¯2)→ℬ​(𝒦1)\rho_{1}:C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}_{1})andρ2:C​(𝔻¯)→ℬ​(𝒦2)\rho_{2}:C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}_{2})that satisfy(2.22)(L1​(z)​(f1​h1))∗=ρ1​(f1)∗​(L1​(z)​(h1))∗and(L2​(z)​(f2​h2))∗=ρ2​(f2)∗​(L2​(z)​(h2))∗\displaystyle(L_{1}(z)(f_{1}h_{1}))^{*}=\rho_{1}(f_{1})^{*}(L_{1}(z)(h_{1}))^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad(L_{2}(z)(f_{2}h_{2}))^{*}=\rho_{2}(f_{2})^{*}(L_{2}(z)(h_{2}))^{*}

for everyz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},f1,h1∈C​(𝔻¯2)f_{1},h_{1}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})andf2,h2∈C​(𝔻¯)f_{2},h_{2}\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}). By Theorem 2.62 in[6], there exist a Hilbert space𝒦3\mathcal{K}_{3}and a mapg:ℍ→ℬ​(𝒦3,ℒ2)g:\mathbb{H}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{K}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{2})such thatδ​(z,w)=g​(z)​g​(w)∗\delta(z,w)=g(z)g(w)^{*}. Set𝒦=𝒦1⊕𝒦2⊕𝒦3\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{2}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{3}. Using (2.21), we can re-write (2) asf12​(z)​f12​(w)∗+L1​(z)​(E​(z))​(L1​(w)​(E​(w)))∗+L2​(z)​(e​(z))​(L2​(w)​(e​(w)))∗+z(3)​w¯(3)​g​(z)​g​(w)∗\displaystyle f_{12}(z)f_{12}(w)^{*}+L_{1}(z)(E(z))(L_{1}(w)(E(w)))^{*}+L_{2}(z)(e(z))(L_{2}(w)(e(w)))^{*}+z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)}g(z)g(w)^{*}=f32​(z)​f32​(w)∗+L1​(z)​(1)​(L1​(w)​(1))∗+L2​(z)​(1)​(L2​(w)​(1))∗+g​(z)​g​(w)∗\displaystyle=f_{32}(z)f_{32}(w)^{*}+L_{1}(z)(1)(L_{1}(w)(1))^{*}+L_{2}(z)(1)(L_{2}(w)(1))^{*}+g(z)g(w)^{*}

for everyz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H}. Consider the spaces given byℒd=span¯​{[f12​(z)∗​vY​(z)∗​[(L1​(z)​(1))∗​v(L2​(z)​(1))∗​vg​(z)∗​v]]:z∈ℍ,v∈ℒ2}​and​ℒr=span¯​{[f32​(z)∗​v(L1​(z)​(1))∗​v(L2​(z)​(1))∗​vg​(z)∗​v]:z∈ℍ,v∈ℒ2},\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{d}=\overline{\text{span}}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}f_{12}(z)^{*}v\\
Y(z)^{*}\begin{bmatrix}(L_{1}(z)(1))^{*}v\\
(L_{2}(z)(1))^{*}v\\
g(z)^{*}v\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}:z\in\mathbb{H},v\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\right\}\ \text{and}\ \mathcal{L}_{r}=\overline{\text{span}}\left\{\begin{bmatrix}f_{32}(z)^{*}v\\
(L_{1}(z)(1))^{*}v\\
(L_{2}(z)(1))^{*}v\\
g(z)^{*}v\end{bmatrix}:z\in\mathbb{H},v\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\right\},

whereY​(z)=[ρ1​(E​(z))000ρ2​(e​(z))000z(3)​Iℒ3]Y(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{1}(E(z))&0&0\\
0&\rho_{2}(e(z))&0\\
0&0&z^{(3)}I_{\mathcal{L}_{3}}\end{bmatrix}. Evidently,ℒd\mathcal{L}_{d}andℒr\mathcal{L}_{r}are subspaces ofℒ1⊕𝒦\mathcal{L}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{K}andℒ3⊕𝒦\mathcal{L}_{3}\oplus\mathcal{K}, respectively. It follows from Theorem2.17thatf31∈S​Aℍ​(ℒ3,ℒ1)f_{31}\in SA_{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{L}_{3},\mathcal{L}_{1}). The proof is now complete.

Finally, we present the Toeplitz corona theorem onℍ\mathbb{H}and conclude the section.

- (3)(3)

there existξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)ℍ+,∇∈C​(𝔻¯)ℍ+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}},\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}}andδ∈ℂℍ+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{H}}^{+}such that for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},∑j=1𝑑​φj​(z)​φj​(w)¯−ϵ2=ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w),\overset{d}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}\varphi_{j}(z)\overline{\varphi_{j}(w)}-\epsilon^{2}=\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w),

The proof follows from Theorem2.18by choosing the spacesℒ1=ℂd,ℒ2=ℒ3=ℂ\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{d},\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{3}=\mathbb{C}, and definingf32​(z)=ϵ,f12​(z)=[φ1​(z)…φd​(z)]f_{32}(z)=\epsilon,f_{12}(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\varphi_{1}(z)&\dotsc&\varphi_{d}(z)\end{bmatrix}andf31​(z)=ϵ​[f1​(z)…fd​(z)]tf_{31}(z)=\epsilon\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}(z)&\dotsc&f_{d}(z)\end{bmatrix}^{t}forz∈ℍz\in\mathbb{H}.

## 3.Realization, interpolation, extension and Toeplitz corona theorems on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}

Forz=(z1,…,zd)z=(z_{1},\dotsc,z_{d})andw=(w1,…,wd)w=(w_{1},\dotsc,w_{d})inℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}, set⟨z,w⟩=z1​w¯1+…+zd​w¯d\langle z,w\rangle=z_{1}\overline{w}_{1}+\dotsc+z_{d}\overline{w}_{d}andkd​(z,w)=1/(1−⟨z,w⟩).k_{d}(z,w)=1/\penalty 50(1-\langle z,w\rangle).The Drury-Arveson space is the Hilbert space of analytic functions on𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}with reproducing kernelkd​(z,w)k_{d}(z,w), e.g., see[17,35]. An analytic functionφ​(z)\varphi(z)on𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}is a multiplier of the Drury-Arveson space if the multiplication byφ​(z)\varphi(z)is a bounded operator on the Drury-Arveson space. A multiplier is said to be contractive if the corresponding multiplication operator is a contraction. The class of contractive multipliers of the Drury-Arveson space is called the Schur-Agler class on the unit ball, and is a proper subset of the Schur classS​(𝔹d)S(\mathbb{B}_{d})whend>1d>1. An interested reader is referred to[6,19,36]for further details on the Schur-Agler class on𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}and the associated realization theorem. A version of Nevanlinna-Pick type interpolation in the setting of Drury-Arveson space to a large class of vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces over𝔹d\mathbb{B}_{d}is studied in[30](also see[31,41], and the references therein). In this section, we study a different subclass of Schur classS​(𝔹2)S(\mathbb{B}_{2}), and provide suitable versions of realization, interpolation and extension theorems on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}. In this direction, we recall the following result from[26].

## Theorem 3.1([26], Theorem 10.14).

A point(z(1),z(2))∈𝔹2(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})\in\mathbb{B}_{2}if and only if(z(1),z(2),0,0)∈ℍ(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},0,0)\in\mathbb{H}.

The above theorem shows that𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}can be embedded intoℍ\mathbb{H}, which allows us to establish the realization, interpolation and extension results on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}through the corresponding results onℍ\mathbb{H}. This shows that the function theory onℍ\mathbb{H}naturally provides a single framework from which the aforementioned theorems on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}can be derived. Motivated by Theorem3.1, we consider𝔐𝔹2\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}={(T1,T2):(T1,T2,0,0)∈𝔐ℍ}.\displaystyle=\{(T_{1},T_{2}):(T_{1},T_{2},0,0)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}\}.

Evidently,(z1)​I,z(2)​I)∈𝔐𝔹2(z^{1)}I,z^{(2)}I)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}for all(z1),z(2))∈𝔹2(z^{1)},z^{(2)})\in\mathbb{B}_{2}. Consequently, the class given byℭ​(𝔹2)={g∈Hol​(𝔹2):‖g​(T1,T2)‖≤1​for all​(T1,T2)∈𝔐𝔹2}\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2})=\{g\in\text{Hol}(\mathbb{B}_{2}):\|g(T_{1},T_{2})\|\leq 1\ \text{for all}\ (T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}\}

is contained in the Schur classS​(𝔹2)S(\mathbb{B}_{2}). It is easy to see that a holomorphic mapg:𝔹2→ℂg:\mathbb{B}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}induces a holomorphic mapg∘θℍ→𝔹2g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}onℍ\mathbb{H}, whereθℍ→𝔹2:ℍ→𝔹2,(z(1),z(2),z(3),z(4))↦(z(1),z(2))\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2},(z^{(1)},z^{(2)},z^{(3)},z^{(4)})\mapsto(z^{(1)},z^{(2)}). Capitalizing this, we present the following realization theorem for functions inℭ​(𝔹2)\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2}).

## Theorem 3.2.

For a functiong:𝔹2→ℂg:\mathbb{B}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}, the following are equivalent:
- (1)(1)

g∈ℭ​(𝔹2)g\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2});
- (2)(2)

f=g∘θℍ→𝔹2∈S​A​(ℍ)f=g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}\in SA(\mathbb{H});
- (3)(3)

(1−f​(z)​f​(w)¯)​k​(z,w)≽0(1-f(z)\overline{f(w)})k(z,w)\succcurlyeq 0for allk∈A​K​(ℍ)k\in AK(\mathbb{H});
- (4)(4)

there existξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)ℍ+,∇∈C​(𝔻¯)ℍ+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}},\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{\mathbb{H}}andδ∈ℂℍ+\delta\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{H}}^{+}such that for allz,w∈ℍz,w\in\mathbb{H},1−f​(z)​f​(w)¯=ξ​(z,w)​(1−E​(z)​E​(w)¯)+∇(z,w)​(1−e​(z)​e​(w)¯)+(1−z(3)​w¯(3))​δ​(z,w),1-f(z)\overline{f(w)}=\xi(z,w)(1-E(z)\overline{E(w)})+\nabla(z,w)(1-e(z)\overline{e(w)})+(1-z^{(3)}\overline{w}^{(3)})\delta(z,w),

whereE​(z)E(z)ande​(z)e(z)are as in (2);
- (5)(5)

f∈U​C​(ℍ)f\in UC(\mathbb{H}).

## Proof.

The part(2)⟹(1)(2)\implies(1)follows from the definition ofℭ​(𝔹2)\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2}), and the implications(2)⟹(3)⟹(4)⟹(5)⟹(2)(2)\implies(3)\implies(4)\implies(5)\implies(2)are consequences of Theorem2.7. It remains to show that(1)⟹(2)(1)\implies(2). Letg∈ℭ​(𝔹2)g\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2})and letT¯=(T1,T2,T3,T4)∈𝔐ℍ\underline{T}=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}. Letα,α1,α2∈𝔻¯\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}}. Then‖Ψ​(α,T2,0,0)‖=‖T2‖=‖Ψ​(0,T2,T3,T4)‖<1\|\Psi(\alpha,T_{2},0,0)\|=\|T_{2}\|=\|\Psi(0,T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1and‖ψα1,α2​(T1,T2,0,0)‖=(1−|α1|2)​(1−|α2|2)​‖T1​(I−α1​T2)−1‖\displaystyle\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(T_{1},T_{2},0,0)\|=\sqrt{(1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2})(1-|\alpha_{2}|^{2})}\|T_{1}(I-\alpha_{1}T_{2})^{-1}\|≤1−|α1|2​‖T1​(I−α1​T2)−1‖\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1-|\alpha_{1}|^{2}}\ \|T_{1}(I-\alpha_{1}T_{2})^{-1}\|=‖ψα1,0​(T1,T2,T3,T4)‖\displaystyle=\|\psi_{\alpha_{1},0}(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4})\|<1.\displaystyle<1.

Therefore,(T1,T2,0,0)∈𝔐ℍ(T_{1},T_{2},0,0)\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{H}}and so,(T1,T2)∈𝔐𝔹2(T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}. Then‖g∘θℍ→𝔹2​(T¯)‖=‖g​(T1,T2)‖≤1\|g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}(\underline{T})\|=\|g(T_{1},T_{2})\|\leq 1, which shows thatg∘θℍ→𝔹2∈S​A​(ℍ)g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}\in SA(\mathbb{H}).

An application of Theorem2.7provides the following interpolation theorem on𝔹2\mathbb{B}_{2}.

there existξ∈C​(𝔻¯2)F+\xi\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{2})^{+}_{F}and∇∈C​(𝔻¯)F+\nabla\in C(\overline{\mathbb{D}})^{+}_{F}such that forwi=(zj(1),zj(2),0,0)w_{i}=(z_{j}^{(1)},z_{j}^{(2)},0,0)and1≤i,j≤n1\leq i,j\leq n,1−λi​λ¯j=ξ​(wi,wj)​(1−E​(wi)​E​(wj)¯)+∇(wi,wj)​(1−e​(wi)​e​(wj)¯).1-\lambda_{i}\overline{\lambda}_{j}=\xi(w_{i},w_{j})(1-E(w_{i})\overline{E(w_{j})})+\nabla(w_{i},w_{j})(1-e(w_{i})\overline{e(w_{j})}).

## Proof.

The implication(1)⟹(2)(1)\implies(2)follows directly from Theorem3.2by choosingf=g∘θℍ→𝔹2f=g\circ\theta_{\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{B}_{2}}. The equivalence of(2)(2)with(3)−(4)(3)-(4)follows from Theorem2.8. It is only left to prove(2)⟹(1)(2)\implies(1). Thus,g∈ℭ​(𝔹2)g\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{B}_{2})andg​(zj)=λjg(z_{j})=\lambda_{j}for1≤j≤n1\leq j\leq n.

Recall that a subsetWWofℍ\mathbb{H}is said to have the extension property inS​A​(ℍ)SA(\mathbb{H})if for every non-zerof∈ℋ​ℰ​(W)f\in\mathscr{HE}(W), there exists a norm-preserving extensiong∈H∞​(ℍ)g\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{H})offfsuch thatg/‖f‖∞,W∈S​A​(ℍ)g/\penalty 50\|f\|_{\infty,W}\in SA(\mathbb{H}).
